        section_label: Section 2
        extraction_method: text
        text: 'How To Get A Money Transmitter License (MTL) in the U.S.


          Published Date  January 29, 2026


          To get a Money Transmitter License (MTL), businesses must undergo a rigorous
          application process that involves dual compliance with federal (like FinCEN''s
          AML/KYC protocols) and varying state-level regulations. Key requirements
          across states include demonstrating financial viability, often requiring
          a surety bond and capital reserves, passing comprehensive background checks
          for key personnel, and establishing a robust internal compliance program.
          The process is complex, time-consuming, and mandates extensive documentation
          and financial disclosures to ensure the applicant is law-abiding and financially
          sound.


          Operating a money transfer service in the U.S. involves tackling a myriad
          of federal and state laws, making how to get a money transmitter license
          both time-consuming and expensive. Compliance with agencies like FinCEN
          is non-negotiable to prevent illegal activities.


          Companies can either secure their own MSB license or partner with a licensed
          entity, but legal adherence is vital in both scenarios. It''s essential
          to understand what a money transmitter license is and who needs it.


          What is a Money Transmitter License?


          A Money Transmitter License is an official authorization or money service
          business license that empowers a firm or individual to facilitate the movement
          of capital. This license enables MSBs to handle financial transactions,
          ranging from digital payments to wire transfers.


          Possessing a Money Transmitter License brings forth substantial regulatory
          oversight. Organizations must prove they have the governance structure and
          internal controls to manage and safeguard client funds. States will continually
          scrutinize applicants, denying licensure to those who fail to meet the stringent
          criteria.


          In the United States, the need for a Money Transmitter License is not a
          choice; it''s a requirement for virtually any business involved in the handling
          or transferring of funds. Money transmitter license requirements by state
          can vary, so it''s crucial to be aware of the respective state money transmitter
          regulations.


          How to Get a Money Transmitter License?


          The license application process is designed to be rigorous, ensuring that
          approved businesses are law-abiding and financially sound. Federal and state-level
          regulations diverge, with each state setting its own financial criteria
          and often requiring a minimum capital commitment.


          Applicants typically need a surety bond of at least $500,000 per state,
          with costs influenced by credit history. The duration to obtain the license
          varies, contingent on the business type and the state of operation.


          Money Transmitter License Requirements


          Acquiring a money transmitter license involves compliance with Anti-Money
          Laundering (AML) and Know Your Customer (KYC) protocols. While these regulations
          are federal, individual states often impose additional requirements, thereby
          necessitating dual compliance.


          Subsequent to application submission, state authorities undertake an investigative
          review, focusing on financial sustainability and the applicant''s background.


          Standardized Requirements Across States


          Registration: Initial registration typically occurs with the Financial Crimes
          Enforcement Network or a similar state-level body.'

      source:SRC-37ee676da5b354ba8538:
        kind: source
        record_id: SRC-37ee676da5b354ba8538
        path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/source-library/SRC-37ee676da5b354ba8538/dd8e38dc121ab8ed7fe079b02c7e158a/pages/p000031.md

        file_revision: 994d5382d8c2640923eca8c8aabbca98c8fbaeec037edafbf7675c1a3819b1e7
        source_class: retrieved_source
        availability: selected_passage
        reported: false
        passages:
        - unit_id: p000014
          start: 0
          end: 1963
          page_number: 14
          section_label: null
          body_hash: 5b5d2f6c388b9bcf98252d205bb001dbdce26951229e0e6896dfecf0358be545
          path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/source-library/SRC-37ee676da5b354ba8538/dd8e38dc121ab8ed7fe079b02c7e158a/pages/p000014.md
          text: "14 \n \nARTICLE V \nMoney Transmission Licenses \nSection 5.01 License
            Required. \n(a) \nA person may not engage in the business of money transmission
            or \nadvertise, solicit, or hold itself out as providing money transmission
            unless the person is licensed \nunder this [Act]; \n(b) \n[Section 5.01(a)]
            does not apply to: \n(1) \nA person that is an authorized delegate of
            a person licensed under \nthis [Act] acting within the scope of authority
            conferred by a written contract with the \nlicensee; or \n(2) \nA person
            that is exempt pursuant to [Section 3.01] and does not \nengage in money
            transmission outside the scope of such exemption. \n(c) \nA license issued
            under [Section 5.05] is not transferable or assignable. \nSection 5.02
            Consistent State Licensing. \n(a) \nTo establish consistent licensing
            between [this state] and other states, the \n[Commissioner] is authorized
            and encouraged to: \n(1) \nimplement all licensing provisions of this
            [Act] in a manner that is \nconsistent with other states that have adopted
            this [Act]or multistate licensing processes; \nand \n(2) \nparticipate
            in nationwide protocols for licensing cooperation and \ncoordination among
            state regulators provided that such protocols are consistent with this
            \n[Act]. \n(b) \nIn order to fulfill the purposes of this [Act], the [Commissioner]
            is \nauthorized and encouraged to establish relationships or contracts
            with NMLS or other entities \ndesignated by NMLS to enable the [Commissioner]
            to: \n(1) \ncollect and maintain records; \n(2) \ncoordinate multistate
            licensing processes and supervision processes; \n(3) \nprocess fees; and
            \n(4) \nfacilitate communication between [state] and licensees or other
            \npersons subject to this [Act]. \n(c) \nThe [Commissioner] is authorized
            and encouraged to utilize NMLS for all \naspects of licensing in accordance
            with this [Act], including but not limited to, license applications, \napplications
            for acquisitions of control, surety bonds, reporting, criminal history
            background\n"
        - unit_id: p000021
          start: 0
          end: 2389
          page_number: 21
          section_label: null
          body_hash: 5638ae03ab2c6c110a80e30639b8ccd42a1c585fdc2818a38e4f2f0fc69a5e06
          path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/source-library/SRC-37ee676da5b354ba8538/dd8e38dc121ab8ed7fe079b02c7e158a/pages/p000021.md
          text: "21 \n \nin a medium prescribed by the [Commissioner]. The renewal
            report must state or contain a \ndescription of each material change in
            information submitted by the licensee in its original license \napplication
            which has not been reported to the [Commissioner]. \n(c) \nThe [Commissioner]
            for good cause may grant an extension of the renewal \ndate. \n(d) \nThe
            [Commissioner] is authorized and encouraged to utilize NMLS to \nprocess
            license renewals provided that such functionality is consistent with this
            [Section 5.06]. \nSection 5.07 Maintenance of License. \n(a) \nIf a licensee
            does not continue to meet the qualifications or satisfy the \nrequirements
            that apply to an applicant for a new money transmission license, the [Commissioner]
            \nmay suspend or revoke the licensee's license in accordance with the
            procedures established by this \n[Act] or other applicable state law for
            such suspension or revocation. \n(b) \nAn applicant for a money transmission
            license must demonstrate that it \nmeets or will meet, and a money transmission
            licensee must at all times meet, the requirements in \n[Sections 10.01,
            10.02, and 10.03] of this [Act]. \nARTICLE VI \nAcquisition of Control
            and Change of Key Individual \nSection 6.01 Acquisition of Control. \n(a)
            \nAny person, or group of persons acting in concert, seeking to acquire
            control \nof a licensee shall obtain the written approval of the [Commissioner]
            prior to acquiring control. \nAn individual is not deemed to acquire control
            of a licensee and is not subject to these acquisition \nof control provisions
            when that individual becomes a key individual in the ordinary course of
            \nbusiness. \n(b) \nA person, or group of persons acting in concert, seeking
            to acquire control \nof a licensee shall, in cooperation with the licensee:
            \n(1) \nSubmit an application in a form and in a medium prescribed by
            the \n[Commissioner]; and \n(2) \nSubmit a nonrefundable fee of [$X] with
            the request for approval. \n(c) \nUpon request, the [Commissioner] may
            permit a licensee or the person, or \ngroup of persons acting in concert,
            to submit some or all information required by the \n[Commissioner] pursuant
            to [Section 6.01(b)(1)] without using NMLS. \n(d) \nThe application required
            by [Section 6.01(b)(1)] shall include information \nrequired by [Section
            5.04] for any new key individuals that have not previously completed the
            \nrequirements of [Section 5.04] for a licensee.\n"
        - unit_id: p000022
          start: 0
          end: 2612
          page_number: 22
          section_label: null
          body_hash: 175b57e812c71a8532569030d2db7113b5adc94e61bd9922dc0fee5d833c2748
          path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/source-library/SRC-37ee676da5b354ba8538/dd8e38dc121ab8ed7fe079b02c7e158a/pages/p000022.md
          text: "22 \n \n(e) \nWhen an application for acquisition of control under
            this [Section 6.01] \nappears to include all the items and address all
            of the matters that are required, the application shall \nbe considered
            complete and the [Commissioner] shall promptly notify the applicant in
            a record of \nthe date on which the application was determined to be complete
            and:15 \n \n(1) \nThe [Commissioner] shall approve or deny the application
            within 60 \ndays after the completion date; or \n(2) \nif the application
            is not approved or denied within 60 days after the \ncompletion date:
            \n(A) \nthe application is approved; and \n(B) \nthe person, or group
            of persons acting in concert, are not \nprohibited from acquiring control.
            \n(3) \nThe [Commissioner] may for good cause extend the application \nperiod.
            \n(f) \nA determination by the [Commissioner] that an application is complete
            and \nis accepted for processing means only that the application, on its
            face, appears to include all of the \nitems and address all of the matters
            that are required, and is not an assessment of the substance of \nthe
            application or of the sufficiency of the information provided. \n(g) \nWhen
            an application is filed and considered complete under [Section \n6.01(e)],
            the [Commissioner] shall investigate the financial condition and responsibility,
            financial \nand business experience, character, and general fitness of
            the person, or group of persons acting in \nconcert, seeking to acquire
            control. The [Commissioner] shall approve an acquisition of control \npursuant
            to this [Section 6.01] if the [Commissioner] finds that all of the following
            conditions have \nbeen fulfilled: \n(1) \nThe requirements of subsections
            (b) and (d) have been met, as \napplicable; and \n(2) \nthe financial
            condition and responsibility, financial and business \nexperience, competence,
            character, and general fitness of the person, or group of persons \nacting
            in concert, seeking to acquire control; and the competence, experience,
            character, \nand general fitness of the key individuals and persons that
            would be in control of the \nlicensee after the acquisition of control
            indicate that it is in the interest of the public to \npermit the person,
            or group of persons acting in concert, to control the licensee. \n(h)
            \nIf an applicant avails itself or is otherwise subject to a multistate
            licensing \n \n15 Commentary: NMLS will accommodate and streamline the
            process of communicating when applications are \nreceived and when states
            consider them to have been complete. The intent of this Act is for the
            application period to \nbegin on the day an application is determined
            to be complete.\n"
        - unit_id: p000023
          start: 0
          end: 2523
          page_number: 23
          section_label: null
          body_hash: 83a664c778fbc9de0b4d13270e2a918f6488b461db1a6617e3d36032163068ca
          path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/source-library/SRC-37ee676da5b354ba8538/dd8e38dc121ab8ed7fe079b02c7e158a/pages/p000023.md
          text: "23 \n \nprocess: \n(1) \nthe [Commissioner] is authorized and encouraged
            to accept the \ninvestigation results of a lead investigative state for
            the purpose of [Section 6.01(g)] if the \nlead investigative state has
            sufficient staffing, expertise, and minimum standards; or \n(2) \nif [state]
            is a lead investigative state, the [Commissioner] is \nauthorized and
            encouraged to investigate the applicant pursuant to [Section 6.01(g)]
            and \nthe timeframes established by agreement through the multistate licensing
            process. \n(i) \nThe [Commissioner] shall issue a formal written notice
            of the denial of an \napplication to acquire control within thirty (30)
            days of the decision to deny the application. The \n[Commissioner] shall
            set forth in the notice of denial the specific reasons for the denial
            of the \napplication. An applicant whose application is denied by the
            [Commissioner] under this [Section \n6.01(i)] may appeal within thirty
            (30) days16 after receipt of the written notice of the denial \n[pursuant
            to state administrative law procedures]. \n(j) \nThe requirements of [Section
            6.01(a) and (b)] do not apply to any of the \nfollowing: \n(1) \nA person
            that acts as a proxy for the sole purpose of voting at a \ndesignated
            meeting of the shareholders or holders of voting shares or voting interests
            of a \nlicensee or a person in control of a licensee; \n(2) \nA person
            that acquires control of a licensee by devise or descent; \n(3) \nA person
            that acquires control of a licensee as a personal \nrepresentative, custodian,
            guardian, conservator, or trustee, or as an officer appointed by a \ncourt
            of competent jurisdiction or by operation of law; \n(4) \nA person that
            is exempt under [Section 3.01(g)]; \n(5) \nA person that the [Commissioner]
            determines is not subject to \n[Section 6.01(a)] based on the public interest;
            \n(6) \nA public offering of securities of a licensee or a person in control
            of \na licensee; or \n(7) \nAn internal reorganization of a person in
            control of the licensee \nwhere the ultimate person in control of the
            licensee remains the same. \n(k) \nPersons in [Sections 6.01(j)(2), (3),
            (4), (6), (7)] in cooperation with the \nlicensee shall notify the [Commissioner]
            within [15] days after the acquisition of control.17 \n \n16 Commentary:
            Administrative procedures for challenging a departmental decision may
            be derived from other \naspects of state law. In such a case, this Act
            should be adjusted to reflect the relevant administrative process. \n17
            Commentary: Notice is satisfied upon updating the licensee’s NMLS record.\n"
        - unit_id: p000024
          start: 0
          end: 2799
          page_number: 24
          section_label: null
          body_hash: 4732d9c2b053975a4d3cb27db8e99faa4baf45f71eb350fa519e31b5890ac680
          path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/source-library/SRC-37ee676da5b354ba8538/dd8e38dc121ab8ed7fe079b02c7e158a/pages/p000024.md
          text: "24 \n \n(l) \nStreamlined Acquisition of Control \n(1) \nThe requirements
            of [Section 6.01(a) and (b)] do not apply to a \nperson that has complied
            with and received approval to engage in money transmission \nunder this
            [Act] or was identified as a person in control in a prior application
            filed with and \napproved by the [Commissioner] or by an MSB accredited
            state pursuant to a multistate \nlicensing process, provided that: \n(A)
            \nthe person has not had a license revoked or suspended or \ncontrolled
            a licensee that has had a license revoked or suspended while the person
            \nwas in control of the licensee in the previous 5-years; \n(B) \nif the
            person is a licensee, the person is well managed and \nhas received at
            least a satisfactory rating for compliance at its most recent \nexamination
            by an MSB accredited state if such rating was given; \n(C) \nthe licensee
            to be acquired is projected to meet the \nrequirements of [Sections 10.01,
            10.02, and 10.03] of this [Act] after the acquisition \nof control is
            completed, and if the person acquiring control is a licensee, that \nlicensee
            is also projected to meet the requirements of [Sections 10.01, 10.02,
            and \n10.03] of this [Act] after the acquisition of control is completed;
            \n(D) \nthe licensee to be acquired will not implement any material \nchanges
            to its business plan as a result of the acquisition of control, and if
            the person \nacquiring control is a licensee, that licensee also will
            not implement any material \nchanges to its business plan as a result
            of the acquisition of control;  and \n(E) \nthe person provides notice
            of the acquisition in cooperation \nwith the licensee and attests to [Section
            6.01(l)(1)(A), (B), (C), and (D)] in a form \nand in a medium prescribed
            by the [Commissioner]. \n(2) \nIf the notice is not disapproved within
            30 days after the date on \nwhich the notice was determined to be complete,
            the notice is deemed approved. \n(m) \nBefore filing an application for
            approval to acquire control of a licensee a \nperson may request in writing
            a determination from the [Commissioner] as to whether the person \nwould
            be considered a person in control of a licensee upon consummation of a
            proposed \ntransaction. If the [Commissioner] determines that the person
            would not be a person in control of \na licensee, the proposed Person
            and transaction is not subject to the requirements of [Section \n6.01(a)
            and (b)] of this [Act]. \n(n) \nIf a multistate licensing process includes
            a determination pursuant to \n[Section 6.01(m)] and an applicant avails
            itself or is otherwise subject to the multistate licensing \nprocess:
            \n(1) \nThe [Commissioner] is authorized and encouraged to accept the
            \ncontrol determination of a lead investigative state with sufficient
            staffing, expertise, and \nminimum standards for the purpose of [Section
            6.01(m)]; or\n"
        - unit_id: p000004
          start: 298
          end: 2509
          page_number: 4
          section_label: null

          path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/source-library/SRC-37ee676da5b354ba8538/dd8e38dc121ab8ed7fe079b02c7e158a/pages/p000004.md
          text: "ly for goods or services provided by the issuer or its affiliate
            or franchisees of the issuer \nor its affiliate, except to the extent
            required by applicable law to be redeemable in cash for its cash \nvalue;
            \n(f) \n\"Control\" means \n(1) \n(A) the power to vote, directly or indirectly,
            at least 25 percent of \nthe outstanding voting shares or voting interests
            of a licensee or person in control of a \nlicensee; \n(B) \nthe power
            to elect or appoint a majority of key individuals or \nexecutive officers,
            managers, directors, trustees, or other persons exercising \nmanagerial
            authority of a person in control of a licensee; or \n(C) \nthe power to
            exercise, directly or indirectly, a controlling \ninfluence over the management
            or policies of a licensee or person in control of a \nlicensee. \n(2)
            \nRebuttable Presumption of Control. \n(A) \nA person is presumed to exercise
            a controlling influence \nwhen the person holds the power to vote, directly
            or indirectly, at least 10 percent \nof the outstanding voting shares
            or voting interests of a licensee or person in control \nof a licensee.2
            \n(B) \nA person presumed to exercise a controlling influence as \ndefined
            by this [Section 2.01(f)] can rebut the presumption of control if the
            person \nis a passive investor. \n(3) \nFor purposes of determining the
            percentage of a person controlled \nby any other person, the person's
            interest shall be aggregated with the interest of any other \nimmediate
            family member, including the person's spouse, parents, children, siblings,
            \nmothers- and fathers-in law, sons- and daughters-in-law, brothers- and
            sisters-in-law, and \nany other person who shares such person's home.
            \n(g) \n\"Eligible rating\" shall mean a credit rating of any of the three
            highest rating \ncategories provided by an eligible rating service, whereby
            each category may include rating \n \n2 Commentary: The obligation to
            rebut control rests exclusively with the person acquiring ≥10% of the
            outstanding \nvoting shares or voting interests of a licensee or person
            in control of a licensee.  It is not the responsibility of the \nlicensee
            to rebut control.  This is consistent with Section 601(a) of the Act which
            requires the person acquiring control \nto obtain prior approval.\n"

          text: "30 \n \n(1) \nappoint the person signing the contract as the licensee's
            authorized \ndelegate with the authority to conduct money transmission
            on behalf of the licensee; \n(2) \nset forth the nature and scope of the
            relationship between the \nlicensee and the authorized delegate and the
            respective rights and responsibilities of the \nparties; \n(3) \nrequire
            the authorized delegate to agree to fully comply with all \napplicable
            state and federal laws, rules, and regulations pertaining to money transmission,
            \nincluding this [Act] and regulations implementing this [Act], relevant
            provisions of the \nBank Secrecy Act and the USA PATRIOT ACT; \n(4) \nrequire
            the authorized delegate to remit and handle money and \nmonetary value
            in accordance with the terms of the contract between the licensee and
            the \nauthorized delegate; \n(5) \nimpose a trust on money and monetary
            value net of fees received for \nmoney transmission for the benefit of
            the licensee; \n(6) \nrequire the authorized delegate to prepare and maintain
            records as \nrequired by this [Act] or regulations implementing this [Act],
            or as reasonably requested \nby the [Commissioner]; \n(7) \nacknowledge
            that the authorized delegate consents to examination \nor investigation
            by the [Commissioner]; \n(8) \nstate that the licensee is subject to regulation
            by the [Commissioner] \nand that, as part of that regulation, the [Commissioner]
            may suspend or revoke an \nauthorized delegate designation or require
            the licensee to terminate an authorized delegate \ndesignation; and \n(9)
            \n(9)  acknowledge receipt of the written policies and procedures \nrequired
            under [Section 8.01(b)(1)]. \n(e) \nIf the licensee's license is suspended,
            revoked, surrendered, or expired, the \nlicensee must, within five (5)
            business days, provide documentation to the [Commissioner] that \nthe
            licensee has notified all applicable authorized delegates of the licensee
            whose names are in a \nrecord filed with the [Commissioner] of the suspension,
            revocation, surrender, or expiration of a \nlicense. Upon suspension,
            revocation, surrender, or expiration of a license, applicable authorized
            \ndelegates shall immediately cease to provide money transmission as an
            authorized delegate of the \nlicensee. \n(f) \nAn authorized delegate
            of a licensee holds in trust for the benefit of the \nlicensee all money
            net of fees received from money transmission.  If any authorized delegate
            \ncommingles any funds received from money transmission with any other
            funds or property owned \nor controlled by the authorized delegate, all
            commingled funds and other property shall be \nconsidered held in trust
            in favor of the licensee in an amount equal to the amount of money net
            of\n"

          path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/source-library/SRC-37ee676da5b354ba8538/dd8e38dc121ab8ed7fe079b02c7e158a/pages/p000031.md
          text: "31 \n \nfees received from money transmission.22 \n(g) \nAn authorized
            delegate may not use a subdelegate to conduct money \ntransmission on
            behalf of a licensee. \nSection 8.02 Unauthorized Activities \nA person
            shall not engage in the business of money transmission on behalf of a
            person \nnot licensed under this [Act]  or not exempt pursuant to [Article
            III] of this [Act]. A person that \nengages in such activity provides
            money transmission to the same extent as if the person were a \nlicensee,
            and shall be jointly and severally liable with the unlicensed or nonexempt
            person. \nSection 8.03 [Optional]: Prohibited Authorized Delegates. \n(a)
            \nThe [appropriate court] in an action brought by a licensee shall have
            \njurisdiction to grant appropriate equitable or legal relief, including
            without limitation prohibiting \nthe authorized delegate from directly
            or indirectly acting as an authorized delegate for any licensee \nin this
            state and the payment of restitution, damages or other monetary relief,
            if the [appropriate \ncourt] finds that an authorized delegate failed
            to remit money in accordance with the written \ncontract required by [Section
            8.01(b)] or as otherwise directed by the licensee or required by law.
            \n(b) \nIf the [appropriate court] issues an order prohibiting a person
            from acting as \nan authorized delegate for any licensee pursuant to [Section
            8.03(a)], the licensee that brought the \naction shall report the order
            to the [Commissioner] within thirty (30) days and shall report the \norder
            through NMLS within ninety (90) days. \n(c) \nAn authorized delegate who
            holds money in trust for the benefit of a \nlicensee and knowingly fails
            to remit more than [$XXX] of such money is guilty of a [reference \nto
            state classification] felony. \n(d) \nAn authorized delegate who holds
            money in trust for the benefit of a \nlicensee and knowingly fails to
            remit no more than [$XXX] of such money is guilty of a [reference \nto
            state classification] misdemeanor. \nARTICLE IX  \nTimely Transmission,
            Refunds, and Disclosures \nSection 9.01 Timely Transmission. \n(a) \nEvery
            licensee shall forward all money received for transmission in \naccordance
            with the terms of the agreement between the licensee and the sender unless
            the licensee \nhas a reasonable belief or a reasonable basis to believe
            that the sender may be a victim of fraud or \nthat a crime or violation
            of law, rule, or regulation has occurred, is occurring, or may occur.
            \n(b) \nIf a licensee fails to forward money received for transmission
            in accordance \nwith this section, the licensee must respond to inquiries
            by the sender with the reason for the failure \n \n22 Commentary: An express
            trust account or segregated bank account is not required.\n"

          body_hash: 3c6e0983ad889dc93334add58721659be3ac3d4bf1075f544bcd9c940396e471
          path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/source-library/SRC-71b20ecf65825d6522c4/d021ece0cde82565eb83511c8122c85b/sections/s000001.md
          text: 'ey Transmitter License.


            Access to NMLS


            NMLS is a secure web-based system created by state regulators to provide
            efficiencies in the processing of state licenses and improve supervision
            of state-regulated industries.


            To access NMLS for the first time, you must complete a Company Account
            Request Form and identify a Primary Account Administrator and a Secondary
            Account Administrator. This form can be submitted online through the NMLS
            website in the Getting Started section. This form needs to be submitted
            only once per company, regardless of the number of NMLS participating
            states in which you are licensed.


            Within three days of completing and submitting the Company Account Request
            Form, the Primary Account Administrator will receive NMLS login information.
            The Primary Account Administrator will have full rights to access the
            system, submit information to this agency and other participating state
            regulators, and set up other company users in the system. Instructions
            and tutorials on how to access and use the system are also available in
            the NMLS Resource Center .


            Note : If your company already has a record in NMLS, you can omit this
            step.


            Managing Your License on NMLS


            Each company holding a DFS Money Transmitter License who wishes to manage
            their license on NMLS must create a company record in the system. To gain
            access to NMLS, the applicant must submit a form requesting an NMLS account.


            Current New York State Money Transmitters may transition their licenses
            to NMLS. See the DFS Money Transmitter Transition Checklist for more information.


            Submitting a Complete Application


            To submit an application and ensure your application is received, acknowledged
            as complete, and processed, please follow the instructions on the DFS
            Money Transmitter Checklists . No application shall be deemed to be complete
            until the Department has received all required information, documents,
            and fees.


            In the process of reviewing and considering the application, DFS may request
            additional information and supporting documents.


            In the application process, an NMLS Identification Number will be assigned
            to your application. Please maintain a record of this NMLS Identification
            Number, as it will be used as your application reference number throughout
            the remainder of the application process. The applicant’s NMLS Identification
            Number must be included on every hard-copy document submitted to the Department.


            Note : If the applicant has already submitted Forms MU1 and MU2 through
            NMLS for another state, the applicant does not need to re-enter the company
            record into NMLS. However, the applicant is required to provide jurisdiction-specific
            information to support its New York State application.


            Back to Top ▲


            Next Section


            Continue


            Annual Reporting


            The Annual Report should be prepared by qualified persons and submitted
            within 120 days following the close of the calendar year January 1 to
            December 31, 2025.


            2025 NMLS Money Transmitter Annual Report Form


            2025 Non-NMLS Money Transmitter Annual Report Form


            Back to Top ▲


            Next Section


            Continue


            Reporting Change in Certain Individuals


            Pursuant to the following sections of the New York Banking Law (BL) and
            Superintendent’s Regulations (SR), licensees of the Department of Financial
            Services (DFS) are required to notify the Superintendent of changes in
            certain individuals, within stipulated time frames:


            Money Transmitters: BL 641, 642 and 652-a; and SR 406.11


            These requirements are for changes in certain individuals of licensees
            whose licenses are not managed on NMLS. For those managed on NMLS, visit
            the NMLS section website for New York State and navigate to Company Licenses
            and Amendment under the pertinent industry type, and look under Change
            in Control on the Amendment Checklist.


            Included Individuals


            Members of the governing board such as directors, trustees, and LLC members


            Officers (at a minimum Chief Executive Officer, President, Chief Financial
            Officer, Chief Compliance Officer, and Chief Operations Officer, and any
            other officers deemed necessary by DFS)


            Documentary requirements for Each Included Individual Being Added


            Notification on the licensee''s letterhead signed by an authorized officer
            containing the following:


            Name and title of the individual being added


            Effective date of the addition


            Name and title of the person being replaced, and his/her new title/position,
            if any


            Resolution of the governing board approving the addition


            Personal Questionnaire (PDF)


            Fingerprints


            Background Investigation Report *


            Litigation Affidavit (PDF)


            Authority to Release Information (PDF)


            Resume


            Documentary requirements for Each Included Individual Being Removed:


            Notification on the licensee''s letterhead signed by an authorized officer
            containing the following:


            Name and title of the individual being removed


            Reason of the removal


            Effective date of the removal


            New title/position, if any


            Where To Submit?'

          text: 'How To Get A Money Transmitter License (MTL) in the U.S.


            Published Date  January 29, 2026


            To get a Money Transmitter License (MTL), businesses must undergo a rigorous
            application process that involves dual compliance with federal (like FinCEN''s
            AML/KYC protocols) and varying state-level regulations. Key requirements
            across states include demonstrating financial viability, often requiring
            a surety bond and capital reserves, passing comprehensive background checks
            for key personnel, and establishing a robust internal compliance program.
            The process is complex, time-consuming, and mandates extensive documentation
            and financial disclosures to ensure the applicant is law-abiding and financially
            sound.


            Operating a money transfer service in the U.S. involves tackling a myriad
            of federal and state laws, making how to get a money transmitter license
            both time-consuming and expensive. Compliance with agencies like FinCEN
            is non-negotiable to prevent illegal activities.


            Companies can either secure their own MSB license or partner with a licensed
            entity, but legal adherence is vital in both scenarios. It''s essential
            to understand what a money transmitter license is and who needs it.


            What is a Money Transmitter License?


            A Money Transmitter License is an official authorization or money service
            business license that empowers a firm or individual to facilitate the
            movement of capital. This license enables MSBs to handle financial transactions,
            ranging from digital payments to wire transfers.


            Possessing a Money Transmitter License brings forth substantial regulatory
            oversight. Organizations must prove they have the governance structure
            and internal controls to manage and safeguard client funds. States will
            continually scrutinize applicants, denying licensure to those who fail
            to meet the stringent criteria.


            In the United States, the need for a Money Transmitter License is not
            a choice; it''s a requirement for virtually any business involved in the
            handling or transferring of funds. Money transmitter license requirements
            by state can vary, so it''s crucial to be aware of the respective state
            money transmitter regulations.


            How to Get a Money Transmitter License?


            The license application process is designed to be rigorous, ensuring that
            approved businesses are law-abiding and financially sound. Federal and
            state-level regulations diverge, with each state setting its own financial
            criteria and often requiring a minimum capital commitment.


            Applicants typically need a surety bond of at least $500,000 per state,
            with costs influenced by credit history. The duration to obtain the license
            varies, contingent on the business type and the state of operation.


            Money Transmitter License Requirements


            Acquiring a money transmitter license involves compliance with Anti-Money
            Laundering (AML) and Know Your Customer (KYC) protocols. While these regulations
            are federal, individual states often impose additional requirements, thereby
            necessitating dual compliance.


            Subsequent to application submission, state authorities undertake an investigative
            review, focusing on financial sustainability and the applicant''s background.


            Standardized Requirements Across States


            Registration: Initial registration typically occurs with the Financial
            Crimes Enforcement Network or a similar state-level body.'
        source_version: 6ebc7b743e605b8e05363eb0628942c1

          body_hash: 8ef30c0b98621db1f11c16332c16e9928ea8f237d6b055f645f7b449f18cbdfb
          path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/source-library/SRC-e6c1e4e85e004ccbe413/e06621859d4ec9431f2712a7c357b112/sections/s000001.md
          text: ', 2026


            Posted in Bank Regulatory


            On May 12, the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) issued
            a significant interpretive letter confirming that Fidelity Digital Assets,
            National Association (the recently converted national trust bank formerly
            known as Fidelity Digital Assets Service, LLC) is not required to hold
            state money transmitter licenses to conduct its federally authorized activities.
            The OCC concluded that the National Bank Act preempts any state money
            transmitter licensing requirement as applied to a national bank.


            Until December 12, 2025, Fidelity Digital Assets operated as a New York-chartered
            limited liability trust company and held money transmitter licenses in
            numerous states, including Iowa. On that date, the OCC approved its conversion
            to an uninsured national trust bank under 12 U.S.C. § 27(a). Concurrent
            with the conversion, the Bank surrendered its Iowa money transmitter license.


            Iowa pushed back. Its money transmitter statute exempts banks and trust
            companies from licensing, but only if they hold federally insured deposits.
            Because Fidelity Digital Assets is an uninsured national bank, Iowa argued
            the exemption did not apply and requested a legal basis for the license
            surrender. Fidelity Digital Assets responded that the National Bank Act
            preempts Iowa’s requirements and asked the OCC to confirm that position.


            The OCC’s analysis of whether a state can require a national bank to obtain
            a state money transmitter license as a condition of engaging in federally
            authorized activities, including cryptocurrency custody, trade execution,
            stablecoin issuance, and related digital asset services, rested on two
            independent and well-established pillars of federal banking law:


            1. National Bank Act Preemption


            Federal law authorizes national banks to conduct a broad range of activities
            on a national basis. Any state law that “prevents or significantly interferes”
            with a national bank’s exercise of its federally authorized powers is
            preempted.


            The OCC reasoned that requiring a national bank to obtain a state money
            transmitter license, even as a threshold condition to operating, would
            impermissibly condition the Bank’s federal authority on state approval.
            At the extreme, a state could veto federal authority outright by denying
            or revoking a license. Both outcomes are squarely preempted. This conclusion
            applies to any state’s money transmitter licensing regime, including states
            that limit their licensing exemptions to a subset of national banks (such
            as Iowa’s insured-deposits-only carveout).


            2. Exclusive Federal Visitorial Authority


            Separately, 12 U.S.C. § 484 vests the OCC with exclusive visitorial authority
            over national banks. “No national bank shall be subject to any visitorial
            powers except as authorized by Federal law.” Iowa’s money transmitter
            statute grants the state agency robust supervisory jurisdiction over licensees,
            including the power to demand books and records, conduct periodic examinations,
            take enforcement actions, and even place a licensee into receivership.
            Applying that regime to a national bank would be “fundamentally inconsistent”
            with the OCC’s exclusive visitorial powers and is therefore impermissible
            on independent grounds.


            Together, these two bases lead to a clean and sweeping conclusion: a national
            bank may conduct its federally authorized activities in any state without
            a state money transmitter license, regardless of whether it qualifies
            for any state-law exemption.


            Our Take


            The OCC’s conclusion here was entirely expected. The preemption framework
            for national banks is well-settled, and the OCC’s letter largely restates
            principles the Supreme Court and federal courts have applied for decades.
            That said, one nuance in this letter deserves closer attention: state-chartered
            trust banks are in a very different position.


            Unlike national banks, state trust companies and state-chartered banks
            do not benefit from National Bank Act preemption. Their ability to avoid
            state money transmitter licensing requirements depends entirely on how
            each individual state defines “bank” or “trust company” in its money transmission
            statute and those definitions vary considerably.


            Print:'
